The alternatives, settled fork by fork, included a narrower per-channel control and a callback with no return value. A user who supplies a callback silently stops getting per-observation predictions back, test predictions included, which nothing else drops; a mistake inside a callback crashes the session with no condition to catch; and an interrupt cannot land while a call is running. The maintainer asked for the work, ruled "Ship the header entry and its two types now.", approved the keep-fits control over every per-observation channel, and picked the integer return with its cancel plumbing, leaving the remaining forks to the recommendations. The costs are one irreversible header entry, a recomputed interface hash, and a control frozen at 1.0. See also: [dec-B62], [dec-B86].
Record: docs/plans/per-draw-callbacks.md; docs/design/per-draw-callbacks.md. Marked: mine. [dec-B114]

**Within-chain threading closes; the thread count gets honest**
Within-chain threading does not ship: re-measured on the current engine it reached at best 1.03 times the speed anywhere, with four workers at a million observations, and lost about 5 percent at two workers and 15 percent at eight at a hundred thousand, so the prototype is archived rather than revived. The thread count keeps its own meaning as a total thread budget distinct from the number of chains, rather than collapsing onto it, and its default becomes the smaller of a guess at the core count and the number of chains. A budget above the chain count warns once per fit, naming both numbers, that tree sampling uses at most one thread per chain and that the excess reaches only the test-fit pool and prediction's fan-out. The alternative was reviving the opt-in the earlier ruling called for. No single-chain speed lever ships before the release, the prototype's correctness half, byte-identical draws across worker counts, is banked rather than revived, and a caller who asks for more threads than chains gets a warning instead of a faster sweep. The maintainer: "Close it (archive it?). I dislike treating n.threads as n.chains and would like to keep that distinction, but we can simple warn that excess threads won't be used and default to setting n.threads to n.chains." See also: [dec-B89].
Record: docs/plans/engine-performance.md; docs/design/within-chain-threading.md. Marked: mine. [dec-B115]

**Prior settings move onto prior objects**
The front door sheds seven scalar arguments that duplicated slots on the prior objects: the tree prior's power and base and the split probabilities move onto the tree-prior constructors, the leaf prior's scale onto the normal leaf prior, and the residual prior's degrees of freedom and quantile onto the chi-squared residual prior. The mixture over tree moves becomes a control slot that the bridge reads, while the leaf-scale parameter k stays as the one promoted scalar and the supplied residual-scale estimate stays on the data side. The alternatives were leaving these reachable only through the sampler constructor, and promoting four of them to arguments with the tree prior carrying the cap. The retired names are still accepted with a once-per-session warning until 1.1-0, so every example and consumer test written against them now warns; the front door and the cross-validation entry both gain a control argument under one precedence rule, a supplied flat argument beating the control slot, and a control carrying fit-state attributes is refused. That reverses the retired control argument on cross-validation before its removal ever shipped, and the residual prior then moved onto the family. The maintainer: "Great, then let's do it." See also: [dec-B77], [dec-B10], [dec-B123].
Record: docs/plans/front-door-formals.md. Marked: mine. [dec-B116]

**Updating a predictor repartitions in place**
After a predictor update the engine partitions a dense root in place instead of rewriting the identity order, because the span it receives is already partitioned under the live rules, so the scan writes nothing where the rewrite wrote the whole span. Predictor updates run 19 to 37 percent faster at no cost to sampling, on the package's distinguishing path, a sampler driven inside a larger model. The member order differs, so leaf sums reassociate in the last bit and every predictor-update scenario has to be re-recorded; the sparse layout stays non-bitwise, since making it bitwise would cost fits with sparse columns 3 to 7 percent for a property no user observes. The alternative was landing it after the release to avoid that re-record; both directions were measured at the maintainer's request and replicated independently on two machines. The maintainer: "Land it before release."
Record: docs/plans/setpredictor-partition.md; the root TODO's engine constants audit item. Marked: mine. [dec-B117]

**The binary prior study gates the release candidate**
The binary node hyperprior evaluation completes before a release candidate is declared, rather than before 1.0-0 if time allows. Only the timing moved from the earlier ruling: the study design and the shipped default stood throughout. That study reported on 2026-09-14, over 28 priors, 162 simulated cells and 22 real datasets, recommending that chi(1.5, 2) stay, and the maintainer confirmed that default the same day on the study's evidence. The maintainer, on 2026-09-11: "I'd like it to be considered pre-release, and even pre-release candidate". See also: [dec-A07], [dec-B106].
Record: this register. Marked: mine. [dec-B118]

**The drawn variance surface is readable**
A sampler fitting a heteroscedastic model exposes the variance surface it has drawn through a current-state accessor on the sampler object, returning what a run's variance and test-variance channels report and NULL when the sampler is homoscedastic, and prior-predictive draws for such samplers are no longer refused. Both landed with the ruling on 2026-09-13. The two alternatives not taken were rebuilding the surface in R from the reported trees, which duplicates engine arithmetic inside the check meant to test it, and deferring both arms of the simulation-based calibration check, which would ship with heteroscedastic calibration unchecked. A user can read the variance surface a heteroscedastic fit drew and can draw from its prior predictive. Three options were put and the maintainer said "Use option 1".
Record: docs/design/aft-status-setter.md landing note. Marked: mine. [dec-B121]

**The variance forest's leaf prior recalibrates on a rescaling swap**
On a heteroscedastic sampler, a setResponse or setOffset call with updateScale = TRUE, or a whole-data swap through setData, recalibrates the variance forest's leaf prior to the new response scale, reusing the recalibration the engine already performs on a setModel swap. The leaf prior is calibrated once, at creation, on the working scale, and before this nothing rescaled it when a later swap moved that scale: the prior stayed pinned to the old scale while the drawn surface caught up over the following sweeps, and after setData the reported sigma came out scaled by the ratio of the old and new response ranges. The setResponse and setOffset case needs updateScale = TRUE, which is not the default. The alternatives were refusing such a swap on a heteroscedastic sampler, and documenting the staleness without fixing it. A swapped sampler now matches one built fresh on the new response, bitwise in the leaf prior and to rounding in the draws. The maintainer ruled the recalibration in on 2026-09-14, on condition that exactly that identity test pass, with refusal as the fallback, and extended the ruling to setData the same day.
Record: docs/plans/archive/variance-forest-mutation-routing.md, "Doors held open". Marked: mine. [dec-B122]

**The residual prior has one home, and sigest belongs to the chisq one**
The residual scale's prior is reached only through the family object that draws that scale - gaussian(sigma = chisq(df, quant)) or gaussian(sigma = fixed(value)), and likewise on student, aft and hurdle.lognormal - on every entry point. The flat resid.prior argument that the sampler constructors, the specification builder and the cross-validation entry had kept beside the family's setting is retired like the front door's: it warns once per session, the value is still used, and it is gone at 1.1-0. The alternative was the surface as it stood, two homes on the constructors with a silent rule that let the flat argument beat the family's setting, rejected because a reader of a call cannot tell which spelling the fit used. A call that writes the prior both ways is refused where the two disagree, naming both and saying which to delete, and accepted where they agree. sigest stays a flat argument: it is the estimate a chisq prior's quantile is calibrated against, so it is accepted beside chisq and refused beside fixed, which sets the residual scale itself and would have ignored it. A user who wrote resid.prior on a sampler constructor sees one warning and the same fit; one who wrote the prior twice with different values, or sigest beside a fixed scale, gets an error instead of a silent winner or an argument that did nothing. The maintainer ruled both on 2026-09-14.
Record: docs/plans/front-door-formals.md, "the residual prior's one home" landing note. Marked: mine. [dec-B123]

**One predictor argument, and it says where each column lives**
The plain-C specification's predictor part is the struct the shipped header's predict entry already takes, so the R parse and a C caller fill the same struct and pass the same translation and checks into the engine, and the planned creation entry takes it too. Where a column's values sit, in the block of doubles or the block of integer codes, is stated by the caller column by column, which is the rule the engine already uses internally. The alternative was the header's current rule, which lets how the sampler stores a column decide which block the caller's index points into: one fewer field to fill, but the argument no longer describes itself, it would be read against the argument's own column types at creation and the sampler's at prediction, and a caller who guesses a column's storage wrong can silently read the wrong block. Speed is the same either way for every current use; only the chosen rule lets an integer-valued numeric column skip the copy to doubles at the call. A C caller that passes integer codes fills one more array, a mismatch between the caller's storage and the sampler's is converted rather than misread, and no released package changes, since none passes codes today. It lands with the plain-C specification, before the 1.0-0 submission. Two rules were put with their costs and a recommendation, and the maintainer, 2026-09-24: "OK, use your recommendation for this item." See also: [dec-B85].
Record: this register. Marked: mine. [dec-B124]

**Frequency weights join precision weights, by explicit choice**
A weight on a gaussian fit is a precision: the model is y given x normal with variance sigma squared over w, as both releases document and as R's lm reads weights. The package gains frequency weights as a new feature, where a row stands for w identical observations, as brms and rstanarm read weights and as Stata, SAS and statsmodels offer beside precision weights. The two agree on the mean and on every leaf update and differ in how many observations the residual scale is estimated from: the sum of the weights rather than the number of rows. Under the precision reading, count data whose weights do not average 1 gets a residual scale inflated by the square root of the mean weight and uncertainty sized to the rows rather than the observations. Precision stays the default, so no existing fit moves, and a user chooses frequency weights explicitly, on the families where that is a coherent model; which families, the argument's name and shape, and the treatment of non-integer values are settled in a design note first. It lands after the merge to main and before the 1.0-0 submission. The alternatives were keeping precision weights alone with the documentation saying so, and switching the meaning of weights to frequency, which would have moved every weighted fit whose weights do not average 1. The maintainer, whose own reading of weights had been frequency, 2026-09-24: "I think we add frequency weights for models where it makes sense as a new feature, pre-1.0 but post main-merge."
Record: this register. Marked: mine. [dec-B125]

**A fit says what it is through descriptors, not through which parts it kept**
A fit object's components split into two kinds. Descriptors say what the model is: the family as specified, the same family with its settings, the number of forests (1 for a single forest), and, on a family with a residual law, its scale model, constant or forest. Which descriptors a fit carries is fixed by its family, never by run options, and each always holds a value. Draw channels hold posterior draws and may be absent because a run chose not to keep them, and no code infers the model from them: the probability transform, the posterior-predictive draw, the plot and the survival curves read the family rather than checking whether the residual scale or the hazard grid happens to be present. What follows from the family - its link, the engine family, the error law's shape - is looked up from it in one table that stops on a family it does not name, rather than stored beside it. So a fit's family element says student for a Student-t fit and hazard.probit or hazard.logistic for a hazard fit, every fit class answers R's family() with that family and its settings, the error-law element leaves every fit, and the heteroscedastic flag, the only true-or-false component and written only when true, becomes the scale-model token. This reverses the survival design's choice to store the engine's binary token on hazard fits so no reader could apply the wrong link; the lookup that stops on an unknown family guards that more strongly than the old default, which sent any token but logistic through the probit link. The alternatives were keeping the existing markers; making only family() and the internal readers follow the rule; and keeping the engine's token in the family element beside a separate specified family, which left two family fields that disagreed. Code that tested whether the forest count or the heteroscedastic flag was absent, or read the engine's token or the error-law token off a fit, changes meaning; all of these are new in 1.0-0 and no released package reads them. The maintainer, 2026-09-24, after the markers were laid out: "Go ahead. Do all four reshaping tasks now," with the scale model spelled as a token; then, asked what the design should be without holding to past decisions, adopted the specified family in the family element: "Go ahead." See also: [dec-B81], [dec-A17].
Record: this register. Marked: mine. [dec-B126]

**The amplitude rescaling move is removed, and kept on record as an option**
The move that rescaled a forest's amplitude against its leaf values, a step that leaves the likelihood unchanged, is removed from the engine, with the treatment-side switches that never ran and the random-number generator only it used. It had been added to cure a low bias in the causal-forest residual scale's calibration, which it did not cure, and was kept as a mixing gain measured on four seeds while it carried a defect: it multiplied a forest's cached fits and its leaf values separately, so the rounding gap between them compounded and biased every fit with an amplitude, the latent causal-forest treatment effect by 0.027 at full length. Measured with that defect fixed, against the same model without the move, over seven designs at two sample sizes and 20 seeds, it gave at most 1.30 times the effective draws per second on the residual scale or the treatment effect, against a bar of 1.5 set before the run, while costing 4 to 6 percent of every sweep with the fix, where the move as shipped had cost 1.2 to 2.5 percent; the amplitude itself mixed better in some designs, but nothing a user reads did, and without the move the amplitude still settles within a few hundred sweeps. The alternative was keeping the move and re-deriving the cached fits from the leaves after every rescale, as stochtree does after a bulk change. Every fit with an amplitude, the causal forest and the formula route's multi-forest fits, draws differently, and a causal-forest sweep runs 1.2 to 2.5 percent faster. The move stays available should a later mixing experiment call for it: its derivation stays in the design notes, its code in the history, and restoring it requires the cache re-derivation. The maintainer, 2026-09-24, after the comparison: "Remove it, but let's not forget that it's an option in case it comes up again in mixing experiments." See also: [dec-A44], [dec-A72].
Record: docs/plans/forest-cache-drift.md; this register. Marked: mine. [dec-B127]

**NEWS and tombstones cover only what reached main**
The 1.0-0 NEWS section describes changes against the released 0.9-x line, and a tombstone keeps only a name that line had. A defect introduced and fixed during development, a limitation of an earlier development build that was later lifted, and a speed figure measured against an earlier development engine get no NEWS text, and an argument or family token that existed only on the development branch gets no tombstone: resid.dist, dispersion, breaks, max.rows, dart, levelGibbs and prior.scale on the front doors, and the twopart family, are unused-argument or unknown-family errors, and the registry drops from 46 entries to 31. The alternatives were a NEWS entry for the amplitude move's cache defect, in the style of the development-only fixes the section already carried, and keeping the development-only tombstones until 1.1-0. No CRAN user ever saw these names or defects; a user of a development build, such as bartCause's 1.0 branch, gets an ordinary error instead of a pointer to the successor, and neither sister package's development branch passes any of the names. The maintainer, 2026-09-24, on the cache defect: "No entry - this hadn't even been promoted to main," then asked for the section's other development-only text to go; on the tombstones, after the options were put: "Use your recommendation for this decision." See also: [dec-A02], [dec-B98], [dec-B101].
Record: this register. Marked: mine. [dec-B128]

**The response type is detected automatically**
Asking for family "auto", the default, inspects the response: numeric 0/1 data and a two-level factor go to probit, an ordered factor of three or more levels to the ordinal family, and three or more unordered levels to multinomial; when a factor response triggers the choice, the call prints one message saying what it chose. 0.9-x already detected 0/1 data as binary, silently, and fitted a factor of three or more levels as a gaussian model on its level codes. The alternatives were detecting binary only, as 0.9-x did, and refusing three or more levels with a message naming the two families, or detecting without the message. The maintainer kept the detection and the message, and ruled that verbose = FALSE also suppresses the message, since a package calling bart inside its own functions usually passes it already; the fit object records the chosen family either way. The maintainer, 2026-09-26: "Yes, let's do that."
Record: this register; docs/plans/archive/package-review-remediation.md for the original adoption. Marked: mine. [dec-A16]

**The sampler's generator options shrink**
The sampler takes one seed argument, spelled seed, and every chain runs its own Mersenne-Twister generator seeded from a dedicated seeding generator; without a seed the chain generators are seeded from R's stream once, at creation, and sampling never advances R's stream. The rngKind and rngNormalKind arguments are gone, so no argument selects a generator kind and nothing makes dbarts follow another package's stream; rngSeed is accepted with a warning until 1.1-0. The alternatives were an opt-in mode drawing a single chain from R's own stream, through the engine's user-supplied generator arm, and restoring every R generator kind inside the engine. The maintainer kept seed only, and asked that users get clear documentation on how to make runs reproducible. The maintainer, 2026-09-26: "I'm ok keeping seed only, but I want to make sure that there is clear documentation for users on how to make runs reproducible."
Record: this register; docs/plans/archive/bart2-argument-consolidation.md for the original naming. Marked: mine. [dec-A04]

**A new missing value at predict is refused**
Predicting on data whose column carries a missing value where the training column had none is refused by name, since a split learns a route for missing values only on a column that had them when the trees were grown. 0.9-34 did two silent things instead: a data frame passed to a formula fit lost its incomplete rows to R's default missing-value handling, so the predictions came back shorter than the data and misaligned with it, and a matrix sent a missing value left at every split on its column, as if it were below every cut. The alternatives were returning NA for those rows, a fixed documented direction, and averaging over both branches by their training shares. The maintainer kept the refusal as the default and ruled that predict gains an na.action argument, as the fit has one: the default predicts every row with a learned route and refuses the rest, na.pass returns NA for the unroutable rows, na.omit drops any row with a missing value, and na.fail errors on any missing value. The maintainer, 2026-09-26: "Yes, let's do that." na.exclude drops incomplete rows and pads them back as NA, keeping the output aligned with newdata as the fit-time exclude record does; base R's predict.lm treats it as na.omit on new data, which the maintainer set aside for consistency with fitting (2026-09-26: "Let's for consistency with fitting and use (a)"). Every observation-indexed output - predict, extract, fitted and residuals - carries the data's row names on its observation margin, the training side from row names the fit keeps; 0.9-x named none, and predict alone was weighed. The maintainer, 2026-09-26: "No, do (2). predict only doesn't make sense, because we only identified the shortcoming by happenstance." When no row of newdata survives, predict returns the empty or all-NA result with the right draw dimensions rather than refusing; the maintainer, 2026-09-26: "I think it's a little dumb and annoying, but (b) is probably the right call." The argument goes on the functions a user calls on a fit - every predict method and survivalProbabilities - while the sampler's $predict, $predictForests and $getTrees(newdata) keep the default: routable missing values predict, unroutable ones are refused by name. Giving the sampler methods the argument too, and giving it to predict alone, were weighed. The maintainer, 2026-09-26: "(B) seems good" and, after the account of routable versus unroutable input, "Sounds good." A discrete-time hazard fit's training outputs, indexed by person-period rows, are named by R's own make.unique convention - a subject s1 at risk for three periods gives s1, s1.1, s1.2 - as base R row replication and discSurv's long form produce; a subject.period label and repeating the subject name were weighed, and survival's survSplit and BART drop the names. The maintainer, 2026-09-26: "Use (c) then."
Record: this register; docs/plans/prerc-surface-freeze.md for the original refusal. Marked: mine. [dec-B34]

**Every sampler method follows the control's updateState**
A sampler method left without an updateState argument refreshes the object's cached state, the copy that save and load carry, exactly when the control object's updateState says to, whose default is TRUE; this is the 0.9-x rule, restored for the setters. The alternative, which shipped first, made the setters refresh only on an explicit updateState = TRUE while run, the prior draws and growFromRoot followed the control, so one object carried two conventions and a save after setData or a rescaling setter wrote trees that reloaded wrong. A refresh measured about 0.08 ms on four chains of 200 trees, 2 to 11 percent of a sweep at 5000 and 500 rows, and the packages that embed a sampler in a loop already set updateState = FALSE on the control. Flipping the control's default to FALSE, so that saving always needs storeState(), was also weighed and rejected as too much friction for users. The maintainer, 2026-09-26: "I think we just want to go with restoring the 0.9-x rule", having said of the flipped default "it's too much friction for the user and it's easy for me to remember when creating my own samplers."
Record: this register; docs/plans/archive/interface-review.md for the split it replaces. Marked: mine. [dec-A14]

**The first forest is rejection sampled**
The sampler draws its starting forest by sampling whole trees over and over until no leaf is empty, up to ten thousand attempts, which is an exact draw from the tree prior restricted to the trees the moves admit; 0.9-x drew once from the unrestricted prior and collapsed the empty nodes, which maps the rejected mass onto smaller trees. Exhausting the cap raises an error naming the count, reachable only by a bug. Only sweep zero differs from 0.9-x; the prior predictive and the calibration tests rely on the start being an exact prior draw. The alternatives were the 0.9-x collapse and starting every tree as a single root, as BayesTree did. The maintainer kept the rejection draw, to revisit within the mixing research. The maintainer, 2026-09-26: "I think current behavior is correct, but we may eventually need to revisit as part of mixing research." See also: [dec-A12], [dec-A13].
Record: this register; the chain header's prior-draw path. Marked: mine. [dec-A10]

**Zero-weight rows leave the degrees of freedom**
The posterior for the residual variance counts only rows with a positive weight in its degrees of freedom, where the released package added the full observation count. Under precision weights a row with positive weight contributes exactly one power of 1/sigma to the likelihood whatever its weight, and a zero-weight row, having infinite variance, contributes nothing, so the count is the likelihood's own; 0.9-x counted zero-weight rows as observations fitted perfectly and biased sigma low. When every weight is positive the two counts agree and nothing moves. The alternative was keeping the 0.9-x count. The maintainer, 2026-09-26: "The positive weights case corresponds to a correct likelihood, right? If so it should stay." See also: [dec-B120], [dec-B125].
Record: this register; docs/plans/archive/sigma-df-zero-weights.md. Marked: mine. [dec-A11]

**The sampled leaf scale is not capped**
When the leaf-scale parameter k is given a chi hyperprior and drawn each sweep, the draw is exact, with no ceiling. Under an infinite scale the posterior is improper for every dataset: as k grows the likelihood settles at the no-signal value while the prior density keeps growing. A chain with real signal stays anchored, but with weak or no signal k drifts upward, fastest with few trees or a large degrees of freedom, and the fit flattens toward the baseline. Measured without a ceiling, such a chain overflows to an infinite k, every leaf value becomes exactly zero and the run finishes with the baseline fit and no NaN or error. The alternative, which shipped first, clamped the draw at one million with no warning, added when the infinite scale was still the default; it protected nothing the uncapped chain does not reach gracefully, and it could clip draws of a proper posterior at a very large finite scale. Refusing the infinite scale, and a ceiling applied only under it with a warning when engaged, were also weighed. The maintainer kept the infinite scale as the user's choice and ruled the ceiling removed: "Why cap? If a user wants to fit a model and they don't have the data to support it, just let k run wild." The default chi(1.5, 2) is unchanged; the manual states that the infinite scale's posterior is improper and that k can drift to infinity. The maintainer, 2026-09-26: "Yes, record that." See also: [dec-A07], [dec-A10].
Record: this register; docs/plans/archive/chi-k-runaway.md for the ceiling it replaces; docs/plans/archive/chi-default-research.md for the drift under the infinite scale. Marked: mine. [dec-A13]

**Ordered factors get cuts between level codes**
An ordered factor predictor is kept as one column and split at midpoints between its level codes, giving one fewer cut than it has levels, and the per-column cut cap is raised to fit them rather than the grid being thinned; n.cuts does not apply to it. 0.9-x expanded every factor, ordered or not, into indicators, so trees never used the ordering; that expansion stays available through factors = "indicators" and in bartBT. The alternatives were honoring n.cuts by thinning the thresholds of a long ordered factor, and the 0.9-x expansion. The maintainer kept every threshold, finding no sound rule for choosing which thresholds to keep. The maintainer, 2026-09-26: "I don't really see how honoring n.cuts can be done in a realistic way, as there doesn't seem to be a good rule to decide where to put them / what to split. So I think we keep the current behavior." See also: [dec-A06].
Record: this register; docs/design/public-surface.md states the grid, and docs/plans/column-kind-consolidation.md records the consolidation. Marked: mine. [dec-A09]

**Constraint and forest constructors stay off the search path**
interactions, blocks, forest and varianceForest are not exported: like the prior and family constructors, each is in scope inside the arguments that take it, and reachable outside a call through an exported list in the manner of dbartsPriors. A forest() term in a formula works as before. The alternative, which shipped first, exported all four as bare names, against the design document's own rule, putting four ordinary nouns on a user's search path; metafor's forest() is one clash. None of the four reached a release, so the change needs no tombstone. Renaming them with a prefix was also weighed. The maintainer, 2026-09-26: "Yeah, treat them like the priors." See also: [dec-A56]. Because interactions and blocks share their arguments' names, a bare name given as the value, as in a wrapper writing interactions = interactions, takes the caller's variable of that name when one exists, and the constructor otherwise; the alternative was one refusal rule for every vocabulary argument. The maintainer, 2026-09-26: "Yes, it should default to the user's likely intent."
Record: this register; docs/design/public-surface.md for the no-pollution rule. Marked: mine. [dec-A67]

**A second way to constrain interactions**
The package offers blocks() alongside naming groups in interactions(). The two express different priors: interactions(groups) is an allow-list under which a disjoint, total partition makes the fit block-additive with each tree's group drawn by its root split, so the allocation of trees floats with the data, while blocks() assigns every tree to one group permanently with a fixed count per group, guaranteeing each group's representation. The alternatives were merging the two into interactions(), with trees.per.group switching a partition to fixed allocation, and dropping blocks(). The maintainer kept both. The maintainer, 2026-09-26: "Keep both." See also: [dec-A67].
Record: this register; docs/design/interaction-constraints.md. Marked: mine. [dec-A56]

**The fit object's component set varies**
A fit object carries a draw channel only when that fit produced it, so which channel names it carries depends on the family, the number of forests and the run options; the components that say what the model is, such as the family, the number of forests and the residual scale model, are fixed by the family and always hold a value (dec-B126). 0.9-x also varied its components by family and options, sometimes leaving a name in place holding NULL; since $ and double-bracket indexing return NULL either way, only code inspecting names() sees a difference. The alternative was a fixed set of names per family, NULL when not kept. The maintainer, 2026-09-26: "Include only what was produced." See also: [dec-B126].
Record: this register; docs/plans/archive/bart2-argument-consolidation.md tabulates the packaging. Marked: mine. [dec-A17]

**fitted takes the interval level after sample**
The fitted method for a bart fit takes ci.level after sample, so 0.9-x's positional fitted(object, type, sample) keeps its meaning, and the multinomial, ordinal and negative binomial fits gain sample, since each already carries a test channel that extract serves; every class with a test channel reads fitted(object, type, sample, ci.level, ...). A hurdle fit has no test channel, taking no test set, weights, subset or offset, so its fitted stays (object, type, ci.level, ...) until feature completion gives it one. The alternative, which shipped first, put ci.level third on all six classes so that the third position meant one thing, a fork the agents settled without putting it to the maintainer and without weighing the 0.9-x break; outside the package an interval level is a late argument passed by name, as in predict.lm, confint, brms and bartMachine, which carries more weight than one meaning for the third slot. The maintainer, 2026-09-26: "I say move it after sample", and, of the hurdle's missing test channel, "make sure that its missing test channel is treated as part of feature-completion work and we don't forget it." See also: [dec-B07], [dec-A19].
Record: this register; docs/plans/surface-refusals.md, section 8, for the reorder it reverses. Marked: mine. [dec-A15]

**Scalar draws come out through extract, and draws() goes**
extract gains the scalar parameter types its sister packages' methods already carry: sigma, k and varcount on bart fits, thresholds on ordinal fits, dispersion on negative binomial fits and the matching pieces on hurdle fits, a combined vector of draws or, with combineChains = FALSE, a chains by samples matrix, as stan4bart's and bartCause's extract return. The exported draws() generic, added when the posterior package was removed to return an iterations by chains by variables array, is dropped, along with its variable selector; summary keeps the same computation internally. A multinomial fit has no scalar parameter, so its averaged per-category probability is reported by summary alone, labelled plainly rather than as the coined meanProb. tau, the removed grouped model's group spread, leaves the default variable lists. The sampler run method's thread count was removed earlier: in 0.9-x it also chose between R's random number stream and per-chain generators and sized threading within a single chain, and neither survives; a value passed under either old name or in its old position is ignored with a once-per-session warning naming setControl until 1.1-0 (the maintainer, 2026-09-24: "Yeah, remove it for now."). The alternatives were keeping draws() exported with its inert multinomial selector and the dead tau default, or keeping it internal only; stan4bart, bartCause and rstan all pull scalar parameters through extract, and no package in the landscape has a separate draws function. The maintainer, 2026-09-26: "Yes, let's do that." See also: [dec-B99], [dec-A24].
Record: this register; docs/plans/surface-refusals.md for the inert arguments. Marked: mine. [dec-A68]

**The comparison against 0.9-34 is a release gate**
A 26-scenario statistical comparison against an installed dbarts 0.9-34, every changed default pinned on both sides over twenty seeds, runs as part of the release battery at submission, and any scenario that separates beyond the explained differences blocks the release until it is diagnosed. On record, 21 of the 26 agree at the rate the null predicts and the other five trace to decided engine changes: counting only positive-weight rows in the residual scale's degrees of freedom, the empty-leaf veto, no chain carried across cross-validation folds, the repaired acceptance ratio in the change move, and the quantile split points spread over the whole column (dec-B235), which took the quantile row out of the agreeing set on 2026-10-05; that list is kept current as engine decisions land. The per-push and scheduled equivalence checks still compare only against baselines recorded from the new engine, and nothing runs the comparison between releases. The reason first recorded for having no wider check, that deleting the old engine left only a nine-scenario snapshot, was wrong, since 0.9-34 installs beside the branch. The alternatives were leaving the comparison manual and also running it on a schedule in continuous integration. The maintainer, 2026-09-26: "We can add it to the release battery. No need to run it scheduled." See also: [dec-A11], [dec-A12], [dec-B03].
Record: this register; docs/plans/classic-compare.md and benchmarks/R/classic-compare.R. Marked: mine. [dec-B120]

**Test scaffolding is consolidated onto the exported surface**
Before the merge, tests reach the package through its exported functions and the sampler object's methods wherever those can do the job: the internal handle layer in R/bartcore.R, which builds samplers from their pieces and runs and predicts through the raw pointer with its own validation, is folded into the exported path where it duplicates it, and its functions with no caller outside the tests go unless they are truly additive, reaching something the exported surface cannot. The engine's test-only accessors, two of them virtual on the response base class, are held to the same rule. The alternative, which shipped first, grew both layers as the work went, leaving two validation paths for one job that can drift apart and 62 of about 150 test files coupled to internals, the tests exercising a back door rather than what users run. Keeping both through 1.0-0 with the cleanup after the release, and deleting only functions nothing calls, were also weighed. The maintainer, 2026-09-26: "I'd clean up before the merge but we should consolidate what we can on exported functions and leave the internal functions when they're truly additive."
Record: this register; docs/plans/review-2026-08-24/consolidated-report.md, item VD-H. Marked: mine. [dec-A52]

**Test accessors fall under the consolidation**
About thirty accessors compiled into the engine exist only for tests, two of them virtual on the response base class, so the harness shapes the production vtables and object layout. They are held to the rule in dec-A52: an accessor stays only where it is truly additive, and the rest go or move off the production classes. The maintainer, 2026-09-26, as recorded under dec-A52. See also: [dec-A52].
Record: this register. Marked: mine. [dec-A51]

**Downstream memory safety is checked consumer-side**
dbarts runs no continuous-integration job that builds a downstream package under sanitizers. The compiled consumers check the boundary themselves: stan4bart's own workflow, on every push to its development branch, builds dbarts from source under AddressSanitizer and UndefinedBehaviorSanitizer and runs its suite, and treatSens gains the same workflow. At the freeze, stan4bart's sanitizer job is dispatched by hand against the final header, and at the merge both consumers' workflows move their dbarts reference from bartcore to main. The alternative, which landed first and was dropped the same day, was a per-push job in this repository building a pinned stan4bart development branch under sanitizers; the reasons recorded for the drop, paraphrased and not quoted, were that per-push CI building a downstream development branch is not R-ecosystem practice, that the pin rots in the wrong repository, and that a stale consumer already fails loudly at compile or load. A one-off sanitizer run at the freeze and sanitizers in the monthly reverse-dependency smoke test were also weighed. The maintainer, 2026-09-26: "Yes, do that and treatSens too."
Record: this register; docs/plans/archive/capi-dispatch-table.md, the C0 landing note. Marked: mine. [dec-A36]

**A seed argument that is NULL means not given here**
Every seed argument - on bart, xbart, dbarts and dbartsControl - defaults to NULL, meaning not given at this level: at a call, NULL defers to the control's seed, and a control whose seed is NULL leaves the fit unseeded, so set.seed beforehand governs it; an integer seeds. NA is not a spelling of not given: it warns once per session until 1.1-0 on bart, bartBT and xbart, where 0.9-34 defaulted to it, and is refused on dbarts, dbartsControl and dbartsSpec (dec-B137, dec-A118). This is the convention of base R's simulate and of ranger, and unlike a test of missing() it survives a wrapper that forwards its own seed = NULL. It replaces 0.9-34's NA, which meant unseeded in bart and bart2, neither of which took a control, and keep-the-control's-seed in xbart; dbarts had no seed argument in 0.9-34. The call can no longer discard a control's seed; an unseeded fit from a seeded control is reached with a control built without one, or with seed = sample.int(.Machine$integer.max, 1). The alternatives were NA meaning unseeded everywhere, NA deferring everywhere, keeping the split, keying on missing(), and a future-style seed = FALSE for an explicit discard. The maintainer, 2026-09-27, choosing it among those: "Yes, just use your recommendation from before."
Record: this register; the Reproducibility section of bart's manual page. Marked: blank. [dec-A83]

**Row names ride the stored draws**
Row names are attached to the fit's stored draws arrays when the fit is packaged, so extract, fitted and predict hand back named results without copying, and the raw fields such as $yhat.train carry the names too: a formula fit's are the data frame's row names, "1" to "n" by default, and a matrix fit whose x has none stays unnamed. The names are kept in a rowNames slot on the data object rather than inside the predictor container, which stan4bart subsets. This follows base R, whose lm and glm name their raw fitted.values and residuals components. The alternative was naming only in the accessors, leaving the raw fields as 0.9-x had them, at the cost of a full copy of a draws array on every accessor call, since R copies an array to assign its dimnames, and of raw fields that disagree with the accessors. The raw fields change shape against 0.9-x by gaining dimnames, so a comparison against an unnamed matrix fails; no value or index moves. The maintainer, 2026-09-27, choosing between the two: "Yes, keep (a)." See also: [dec-B34].
Record: this register; docs/plans/predict-na-action.md. Marked: mine. [dec-A86]

**A missing value in a predict offset or weight follows na.action**
A missing value in a per-row offset or weights given to predict marks that row incomplete, and predict's na.action governs it as it governs a missing predictor: the default refuses it by name, na.omit drops the row, na.exclude drops it and pads it back as NA, na.pass returns NA for it, and na.fail errors. This matches fitting, where na.omit and na.exclude drop such a row and the default and na.pass refuse it, and base R, whose model frame carries an offset() term under na.action. The landed behaviour, refusing under every na.action, was agent-made after a review found the old silent NA offset row and NaN weight draw; under it a predict call asking to drop incomplete rows errored where the same fit call dropped them. Returning to the silent NA was also weighed. The maintainer, 2026-09-27, choosing among those: "Yeah, (b) makes sense to me." See also: [dec-B34].
Record: this register; docs/plans/predict-na-action.md for the refusal it replaces. Marked: mine. [dec-A89]

**A hurdle fit's k and varcount come back as a two-part list named zero and positive**
extract(type = "k") and extract(type = "varcount") on a hurdle fit return a list with one element per component, following bartCause's per-forest varcount, and the components are named zero and positive, on the list and on the fit's component fields alike. The names follow the R hurdle packages: pscl's hurdle names its parts zero and count, GLMMadaptive's zero_part and main, glmmTMB's zi and cond, brms's hu for the zero probability; count does not fit a lognormal part, and positive is the two-part literature's term. The zero part reports P(y > 0), pscl's orientation and the complement of brms's hu, which the manual states. The alternatives were four type names such as "occupancy.k", refusing both types on the hurdle fit in favour of extracting from each component fit, and an array with a trailing component margin; for the names, keeping occupancy, the ecologists' presence term that no hurdle package uses, and GLMMadaptive's underscored style. The maintainer, 2026-09-27, after asking what use a single array would serve: "I think the list is right, but the names feel odd to me", then "Use (b) for the names." A component whose k is fixed stays left out of the k list, and asking when both are fixed is an error, as landed; that detail is agent-made. Hurdle fits are new in 1.0-0, so the rename needs no tombstone.
Record: this register; docs/plans/extract-scalar-types.md for the list as first landed. Marked: mine. [dec-A77]

**An unseen factor level is always refused**
A factor level in predict's new data that training never saw is refused by name, whatever na.action says, as base R's predict.lm and randomForest refuse it. 0.9-34 built the new data's indicator columns from its own levels and matched them to the training columns by position, so an unseen level was predicted as another level with a column-name warning, or, when the factor also held a missing value, failed on a column count; the branch had refused it by name except when the column also held a missing value, and then sent it down the missing-value route. The alternatives were treating an unseen level as a missing value governed by predict's na.action, and keeping the branch's exception. The maintainer, 2026-09-27, choosing among those: "Missing isn't unknown, (a) is right." See also: [dec-B34].
Record: this register; docs/plans/predict-na-action.md. Marked: mine. [dec-A85]

**combineChains = FALSE always keeps the chain dimension**
When a caller asks extract or predict for combineChains = FALSE, every type returns a chain dimension, of length 1 on a one-chain fit: scalar draws come back as a 1 by samples matrix and per-observation draws as a 1 by samples by observations array, chains first as the maintainer approved for scalar draws. The fit's stored fields are unchanged, a one-chain fit's sigma staying a vector as in 0.9-x. Among packages that expose chains separately this is the norm: rstan's extract(permuted = FALSE), posterior's draws_array as cmdstanr, brms and rstanarm return it, coda's chain lists, and stan4bart keeps a chain of length one (bartCause's bart path stored a one-chain fit's draws without the chain dimension, and now normalizes the one-chain dbarts result on read to keep that shape), nimble's runMCMC alone switching from a list of matrices to one matrix; stochtree has no chain dimension at all. The agent had landed a plain vector for scalar draws at one chain, matching dbarts' own drop of the dimension there, inherited from 0.9-x; a 1-row matrix for scalar types alone was also weighed. extract or predict with combineChains = FALSE on a one-chain fit changes shape against 0.9-34. The maintainer, 2026-09-27, after asking about other Bayesian fitting packages: "Yes, let's do (c)." See also: [dec-A68].
Record: this register; docs/plans/extract-scalar-types.md for the drop as first landed. Marked: mine. [dec-A79]

**A constructor call passed through a wrapper's named argument is not recovered**
A wrapper that takes an argument and passes it on, as in wrapper(interactions(max.order = 1)) where the wrapper calls bart(..., interactions = x), fails with R's own could-not-find-function error plus a hint naming the exported list, dbartsForests$interactions(); the prior and family arguments give the same hint, naming dbartsPriors and dbartsFamilies. The constructors are found only inside the arguments that take them, or through dots a wrapper forwards unchanged. The alternatives were a stack walk that recovers the call from the wrapper's caller, which probes showed silently picking the wrong environment through lapply and Map and on a wrapper argument with a default, and exporting the constructors, which dec-A67 rules out. The landscape: no sister package walks the stack, stan4bart and bartCause evaluating one frame up and treatSens exactly two; base R's lm and glm evaluate their call one frame up and carry the same wrapper failure for subset and weights; rstanarm, mgcv and base glm export their constructors; brms captures prior() unevaluated and documents prior_string() for programmatic use, the shape dbarts takes. The maintainer, 2026-09-27, after the landscape: "Go ahead with your recommmendation for this item." See also: [dec-A67].
Record: this register; docs/plans/constructor-vocabulary.md. Marked: mine. [dec-A75]

**Where the caller's variable is looked up for a shared name**
Under the maintainer's ruling that a bare interactions or blocks given as the value takes the caller's variable of that name (dec-A67), the lookup considers only names used as values in the argument, never names in call position, and stops at the caller's top-level environment: the global environment for user code, the package namespace for code inside a package, so a package's wrapper never picks up a user's global variable and gets the constructor instead. The alternative was to search up to the global environment from any caller, the reach R CMD check flags as a global variable with no visible binding. The cost is that a package function cannot deliberately rely on a user's global through the bare name. The maintainer, 2026-09-27, choosing between the two: "Keep it then." See also: [dec-A67], [dec-A75].
Record: this register; docs/plans/constructor-vocabulary.md. Marked: mine. [dec-A76]

**The sampler's getTrees reads every forest**
The sampler's $getTrees gains a forest argument that, left at its default of NULL, returns every forest's trees with a forest column, forests indexed from 1, as the sampler's other per-forest readers - getCalibration, getForestFits, getForestAmplitudes and getForestVariableCounts - default to every forest stacked; extract(type = "trees") forwards it. Before, $getTrees read only the first forest, with no column saying so and no sentence in the manual, so a multinomial fit's later categories and a causal forest's treatment forest could not be read from R, and the agents had deferred the argument past the release as purely additive, which overlooked that its default would change the silent first-forest answer after release. The alternatives were that deferral and a forest argument defaulting to the first forest, which keeps 0.9-x's output unchanged at the cost of disagreeing with the sibling readers. A single-forest fit's trees gain the forest column against 0.9-x. The maintainer, 2026-09-27: "it makes sense to me to do (b) from your recommendation set", observing that trees are parameters and their reading a modelling question, not a matter of precedent from the calibration reader. The column on single-forest fits was later reversed: see dec-A96.
Record: this register; TODO sampler-gettrees-forest. Marked: mine. [dec-A80]

**Leaf where it means a leaf, node where it means any node**
A user-facing name says leaf when it belongs only to the trees' terminal nodes and node when it means every node. The prior on terminal-node values is leaf.prior on every entry point; the sampler's draw of those values is sampleLeafParametersFromPrior; the sampler's reader and writer of that prior in force, added on the branch as getCalibration and setCalibration, are getLeafPrior and setLeafPrior, and the reader's node.scale.factor and node.scale.divisor columns become leaf.scale.factor and leaf.scale.divisor; gp()'s max.leaf.size and the reader's leaf.model attribute keep their names. Tree structure keeps node: getTrees' rows, one per node of either kind, plotTree's nodeWidth, nodeHeight and nodeGap, and the tree prior's per-node split probability. node.prior and sampleNodeParametersFromPrior shipped in 0.9-x, so each stays as a tombstone until 1.1-0 that warns once per session, names the new spelling and uses the value (dec-A02); stan4bart moves to leaf.prior and bartCause's one getCalibration call follows, in lockstep. Before, the package used node for both: node.prior and the manual's node means were terminal nodes while getTrees' rows and plotTree's sizes were every node. Node is ambiguous in the field too: Chipman, George and McCulloch (2010) write terminal node in full, randomForest's nodesize means terminal nodes and ranger's min.node.size a node to split, while leaf, the word of Hill, Linero and Murray's 2020 review, stochtree and the boosting libraries, never means anything but a terminal node. No package uses calibration as a noun for this prior. The alternatives were node everywhere, leaf everywhere, and stopping at renamed accessors. The maintainer, 2026-09-27, asked for the calibration functions to be renamed, chose leaf, then node when told node is common, then asked whether node is ambiguous ("Node seems like it could be ambiguous, while leaf never is") and settled: "Yes, do it as 'leaf' where it means leaf."
Record: this register. Marked: mine. [dec-B129]

**rbart_vi returns for one release as a port of 0.9-34's**
The variable-intercept fit comes back in 1.0-0, deprecated with a once-per-session warning that names stan4bart, and is removed in 1.1-0. It is 0.9-34's implementation carried over: an R loop that moves the group intercepts into the sampler's offset each sweep, runs one sweep, and draws the intercepts and their spread in R, with the rbart methods alongside it. The alternative was restoring the engine version removed on 2026-09-06, which would have undone about 9,500 lines across 171 files, put two result fields back into the shipped C header and brought back a known mixing defect. 0.9-x scripts that call it run again, at about 0.9-34's speed; the released bartCause is not helped, since it fails on 1.0-0 before reaching rbart_vi and ships in lockstep from its 1.0 branch, and the engine stays free of grouping. The maintainer: "I think we should keep it for 1.0-0 and deprecate it, removing it in 1.1-0", then, choosing between the two, "option 2 is probably best. We shouldn't need engine work. In fact, that it leaked into the engine at all is probably why I said to remove it." This replaces the tombstone half of the removal and the rejection of an R-only grouped intercept for 1.0-0. See also: [dec-A01], [dec-B105].
Record: this register. Marked: mine. [dec-B130]

**Cross-validation can fork its workers or use the caller's cluster**
The cross-validation entry takes a parallel argument choosing between forked workers and separate R sessions, and a cl argument for a cluster the caller already started. The default, auto, forks except on Windows and under RStudio, Positron or the macOS R GUI, and a session option sets the default for every call. The alternatives were keeping separate sessions only, which costs about 80 MB and a startup per worker on every call, and making forks opt-in, which is safe but leaves most users on the slow path. A user on Mac or Linux gets fast startup and shared memory by default; a session that has already used a library that is not fork-safe, such as a multithreaded BLAS, can hang or crash, and the manual names that and the option that turns it off. The maintainer, after asking "why not use forks?" and "why not give xbart an argument to control if it will use forking with some reasonable default behaviors?": "Yes, use your proposal. You can also add cl." See also: [dec-A05].
Record: docs/plans/xbart-fork-workers.md. Marked: mine. [dec-B131]

**Public surfaces follow base R, then similar model-fitting functions**
A public surface - an exported function, a sampler method, an argument or a return shape - copies how base R does the same thing, then how similar model-fitting functions do it; internal surfaces are free. So NULL, not NA, means an argument is absent, and NA is a missing value, as for lm's offset. The first application: NULL is the one spelling of no offset, and an NA test offset is refused on the sampler's methods. The alternative was per-case judgement. A dbarts convention that differs from base R, such as NA standing for none because S4 slots could not easily hold NULL, gives way on the public surface. The maintainer: "public surfaces should try and mimic other functions, base R first and then other similar model-fitting functions. Internal surfaces we can do whatever we want. It's probably the case that NA was used for none because way back when I didn't want to make all the S4 slots unions of null and integer." See also: [dec-A92].
Record: this register. Marked: mine. [dec-B132]

**A multinomial count row with zero trials is accepted with a warning**
A row of counts with no trials enters a multinomial fit contributing nothing to the likelihood, as a (0, 0) row does in glm's binomial fit, and the first such row in a session warns once, at creation or through the sampler's count setter. The alternatives were refusing the row, as nnet's multinom does and as shipped under the standing grant, with the message pointing to subset, na.action and the active-row mask; and accepting it silently. A sampler driven inside a larger Gibbs loop can see counts that sometimes empty without the caller erroring out, at the cost of engine work to make a zero-trial row inert in the multinomial draws. The maintainer: "one could imagine a moving target where 0s might sometimes happen, and it would be very inconvenient if they became errors. We could issue a one-time warning." A count set with every row empty is accepted the same way, at creation and mid-run, as all-zero weights and an all-off row mask already are; the maintainer, after asking "What would we do with all-zero weights?": "Sure." Such a row's residuals follow glm at a zero-weight row, 0 for the default and pearson types and minus the fitted probabilities for response residuals: "Follow glm." Its log-likelihood is 0 in every draw, the exact log-probability and what glm's summed log-likelihood takes from a zero-weight row: "Yep, 0." See also: [dec-A65].
Record: this register; docs/plans/multinomial-zero-trials.md, where a zero-trial row is inactive under the row mask, a mechanism the maintainer confirmed: "the row mask is the right mechanism, or at least the right semantic." Marked: mine. [dec-B133]

**R's family objects are accepted where dbarts has the model**
The family argument accepts base R's family objects and spellings as glm does - the word "binomial", the function binomial, and calls such as binomial(), binomial(link = "probit") and stats::gaussian() - mapping gaussian with the identity link to gaussian, binomial with the probit link to probit and binomial with the logit link to logistic, and refusing any other family or link by name. binomial() alone therefore means logistic, following glm's default link, while dbarts's own default for a 0/1 response stays probit; the manual says so. The alternatives were refusing R's family objects with a message naming the dbarts equivalents, and leaving the misleading refusal in place. A user with glm or stan4bart habits writes the family as they would there, at the cost of a second spelling for three families. The maintainer: "I think we should accept R's family objects where dbarts has the model, and refuse the rest by name." See also: [dec-A27], [dec-B132], [dec-B101].
Record: docs/plans/r-family-objects.md. Marked: mine. [dec-B134]

**Replacing sparse predictors stays sparse**
A sparse replacement on a live sampler goes from sparse to sparse: the bridge hands each sparse column's stored rows and values to the engine, which rebuilds that column's pattern and codes from them, instead of expanding the replacement into a dense block of rows by columns replaced first. The alternatives were accepting that dense peak for 1.0-0 and documenting it, and streaming the dense expansion one column at a time. Peak memory for a sparse replacement then follows its nonzeros, at the cost of a second engine mutation entry beside the dense one. The maintainer, asked why the replacement is densified at all: "Why not stop doing densifying it and just go sparse to sparse?", then "Do sparse to sparse." See also: [dec-A35].
Record: docs/plans/sparse-mutation-direct.md. Marked: mine. [dec-B135]

**sigest defaults to NULL on the modern entry points**
The residual-scale estimate defaults to NULL, meaning estimate it by least squares, on bart, dbarts, dbartsSpec and xbart, as summary.glm's dispersion = NULL means estimate it; an explicit NA keeps that meaning for one release with a once-per-session warning naming NULL where 0.9-34 had sigest (bart; on dbarts, dbartsSpec and xbart, which did not, NA is refused; see dec-A118), and the retired sigma spelling follows. bartBT keeps NA, since its argument list is BayesTree's and BayesTree and BART spell it NA. The alternatives were keeping NA as a silent second spelling, and keeping the NA default while also accepting NULL. NULL, which today fails with "missing value where TRUE/FALSE needed", becomes the documented spelling; a 0.9-x script passing sigest = NA explicitly warns for a release. The maintainer: "Use your recommendation for sigest." See also: [dec-B132].
Record: this register. Marked: mine. [dec-B136]

**seed takes NULL, not NA, for not given**
NULL is the only spelling of an absent seed, as set.seed(NULL) is base R's no-seed and set.seed(NA) an error. On bart, bartBT, dbarts, xbart and dbartsControl, described here as defaulting to NA in 0.9-34 (only bart, bartBT and xbart did; see dec-A118), an NA warns once per session until 1.1-0 and is then refused; dbartsSpec and dbartsValidateComposition, new in 1.0-0, refuse it now, and the manual pages lose their accepted-silently sentence. bartCause and stan4bart, which pass NA into dbarts when unseeded, change to NULL in lockstep. The alternative was accepting NA silently as brms and stan4bart do for their own seeds. The maintainer, after asking "What does set.seed accept?": "Keep NULL as the only \"not given\", and retire NA". See also: [dec-B132], [dec-B136].
Record: this register. Marked: mine. [dec-B137]

**updateState takes NULL to defer to the control**
The twenty sampler methods and updatePredictorPerObservationJointly default updateState to NULL, meaning use control@updateState, with TRUE or FALSE overriding and anything else refused; an explicit NA warns once per session until 1.1-0. The alternative was keeping NA, which format(scientific = NA) supports, adding the validation, and naming it the exception to the public-surface rule. The package then spells defer to the control one way, as seed does, and a NULL or a non-logical no longer fails obscurely or reads silently as FALSE. The maintainer: "Use your recommendation for this item." See also: [dec-B132], [dec-B137].
Record: this register. Marked: mine. [dec-B138]

**The tree-shift step is named treeShift and lives on the control only**
The Gibbs step that shifts each tree's leaves by constants summing to zero across the forest (dec-A23) is set by dbartsControl(treeShift = ), taking "auto" (the default: only for a forest whose structure proposals are all zero), "always" or "never", and is no longer an argument of the cgm() and dart() tree priors. It shipped in development as levelGibbs, a tri-state logical with NA as automatic, settable on the control and on the first forest's tree prior, which only copied it to the control; that name, encoding and second route were agent choices with no register entry. The alternatives were keeping the name, and keeping the prior route. The name says what the step does rather than naming its mechanism beside factor levels and multinomial level centering, the three values read as xbart's parallel = "auto" does, and the per-forest look of the prior argument, which was never per forest, goes. It never shipped, so no tombstone. The maintainer: "treeShift is fine, the rename is fine", asking "Is there any argument to keep it with cgm and dart?" See also: [dec-A23], [dec-B132].
Record: this register. Marked: mine. [dec-B139]

**The rest of the NA-as-none sweep follows the public-surface rule**
Six further applications of dec-B132: nbinom's dispersion defaults to NULL for estimate it, as summary.glm's dispersion and mgcv's nb do; the sampler's run takes NULL burn-in and sample counts for use the control; dbartsControl's n.samples takes NULL for not set; the leaf and tree prior constructors refuse NA where NULL is their default; xbart's 0.9-34 sigma gets its one-release tombstone (the test setters' updateState, which 0.9-34 listed only in its manual, was dropped instead; see dec-A118); and gaussian(link = identity) with an unquoted link reads as glm reads it (dbarts's own gaussian later lost its link under dec-A102; R's stats::gaussian(link = identity) still reads so). An NA that 0.9-34 documented warns once per session until 1.1-0, and one that never shipped is refused. The alternative, per site, was keeping NA as a second spelling. The maintainer, shown the six with their costs: "Go ahead, those look good." See also: [dec-B132], [dec-B136], [dec-B137], [dec-B138], [dec-B139].
Record: docs/plans/na-as-none.md. Marked: mine. [dec-B140]

**Fit-time na.exclude pads on every fit class**
Under the maintainer's ruling that every observation-indexed output carries row names (dec-B34), fitted and residuals on multinomial, ordinal, negative binomial and hurdle fits pad rows dropped by na.exclude back as NA, as the plain bart class already did and as base R's lm does, so na.exclude means the same on every fit class. The alternative was to leave those classes unpadded and record a TODO, under which na.exclude acted as na.omit there and outputs came back shorter than the data they are named after. The cost is one more stored field per fit, the record of dropped rows. The maintainer, 2026-09-27, choosing between the two: "Pad on every class."
Record: this register; docs/plans/predict-na-action.md. Marked: mine. [dec-A84]

**A formula hazard fit pads na.exclude rows**
A discrete-time hazard fit through a formula rebuilds a dropped subject's person-period rows from its time and pads them under na.exclude, as the matrix path does; the matrix path builds its period grid from the kept subjects only, so the two agree and a dropped subject no longer shapes the grid. This is what the standard R workflow gives: expanding with survival's survSplit, discSurv's dataLong or pammtools' as_ped copies a subject's covariates onto every period row, and a glm or gam on the long data under na.exclude pads each of them, the same count and positions (checked on one design, 78 fitted values with 6 NA both ways). The alternative was to let na.exclude act as na.omit on the formula path and document it. The cost is a second evaluation of the model frame when rows drop. The maintainer, 2026-09-27, after asking whether it is the standard users would expect: "Go ahead with (a) then."
Record: this register; docs/plans/predict-na-action.md. Marked: mine. [dec-A87]

**varcount is offered on every fit class**
extract(type = "varcount") works on multinomial, ordinal and negative binomial fits as well as the plain bart class, reading dec-A68's "bart fits" as every fit bart() returns: draws by predictors on the plain, ordinal and negative binomial classes, with a trailing category margin on a multinomial fit, and a leading chain margin under combineChains = FALSE; the hurdle fit returns its zero and positive list (dec-A77). The alternative was the plain class only, under which the other classes' users would reach the counts through the fit's fields. The cost is one more documented shape, the multinomial one. The maintainer, 2026-09-27, choosing between the two: "(a) - no reason not to be consistent with features."
Record: this register; docs/plans/extract-scalar-types.md. Marked: mine. [dec-A78]

**xbart seeds its workers from the caller's draws**
xbart's workers never draw from R's generator: the caller draws every per-unit seed in its own process, under whatever RNGkind() the user has set, and hands each sampler its seed through the control, whose seed path derives the chains' generators from a dbarts generator without reading R's stream; sampling itself runs on the chains' own generators, and the fold splits are already drawn in the caller. A user-supplied generator therefore works at any thread count and gives the same results at every one, and the step that sent the caller's RNGkind() to each worker goes. The agent had made xbart refuse more than one worker under a user-supplied generator, which a default call on a multicore machine reached, since n.threads defaults to the core count; running workers on R's default generator with results that then depend on the thread count, and falling back to one worker with a warning, were also weighed. The idea of seeding workers from the caller's draws is the maintainer's. xbart's draws under a given seed move once, and a user's loss function that draws random numbers draws on the worker's default generator, which the manual states. The maintainer, 2026-09-27: "Could we pre-seed somehow with the draws from the original, single threaded caller?", then "Do it."
Record: this register; the Reproducibility section of bart's manual page. Marked: mine. [dec-A90]

**A k chain that reaches infinity is summarized as is**
With k uncapped (dec-A13), a chain under an infinite prior scale can drift to astronomically large k or overflow to k = Inf; summary reports whatever the arithmetic gives, a mean of Inf, a standard deviation of NaN or Inf and an R-hat of NA or far above 1, and the manual's chi() entry explains the symptoms. On one design, pure noise with one tree under chi(20, Inf), k reached about 1e154, the summary's k row read sd Inf, R-hat 2.35 and a tail effective sample size of NA, and the standing non-convergence note printed. The alternatives were labelling the row as diverged in summary, with a line saying the fit has collapsed to the baseline, and a warning at fit time when a chain's k reaches infinity. The maintainer, 2026-09-27, choosing among those: "(a) is fine."
Record: this register. Marked: mine. [dec-A82]

**dbartsValidateComposition restores the caller's stream**
dbartsValidateComposition(seed = ) restores R's random number stream when it returns, as base R's simulate(seed = ) does and as a seeded bart or xbart leaves the caller's stream untouched; before, it set the seed and left the stream wherever the run ended, so later draws in the session depended on having called it. An unseeded call draws from and advances the caller's stream. The alternative was to document the side effect. The manual's seed entry states the restore. The maintainer, 2026-09-27, choosing between the two: "Yes, do (a)."
Record: this register; the seed entry of dbartsValidateComposition's manual page. Marked: mine. [dec-A88]

**Errors unwind instead of jumping**
A C++ exception thrown inside a callback is caught at the call and rethrown only after the callback's own frame has returned, the jump being made under R's unwind protection so that it unwinds through that frame rather than across it; an exception the engine itself raises travels the same path, and either becomes an R error only at the bridge entry point, once the unwind has run. No raw error call and no long jump leaves engine or callback code, and the same change fixes three sites that leaked heap memory. A host written in C++ still may not jump to a saved position of its own from inside a callback: only raising an R error or throwing is safe. This is the mechanism for now. Returning a status from every entry point instead of raising, the host-neutral shape a non-R host such as a Python binding needs, is scheduled with the host-neutral engine after the merge and before 1.0-0 (dec-B85), and covers every entry point, not the creation entry alone; it replaces only the last step, the unwind to the entry point staying. The alternatives were that status contract now, a header break for stan4bart and treatSens ahead of the scheduled work, and the raw jump that leaked. The maintainer, 2026-09-27, after asking whether the status contract is the more correct one and scheduled for later: "Yes, let's do that."
Record: inst/include/dbarts/dbarts.h; src/R_interface_bartcore_common.hpp; TODO host-neutral engine. Marked: mine. [dec-B119]

**Engine test accessors move behind a test peer**
The engine's test-only accessors, about thirty of them, leave the production classes' public surface for a test-peer structure: each engine class declares that structure a friend, the accessors become private or move onto the peer, and only the C++ tests call through it, so the compiler rather than a convention keeps production code off them. The two virtual hooks on the response base class had already come off it (the earlier agent-made step, since only virtual members shape the production vtables and object layout, dec-A51). The alternatives were stopping at that step, leaving the non-virtual accessors in place under the maintainer's words "leave the internal functions when they're truly additive"; enforcing their ForTesting suffix with a CI check; and compiling them only in the test build under a preprocessor guard. The cost is about 130 test call sites changed once and one friend line per engine class; the compiled package does not change. The maintainer, 2026-09-27, on the enforcement options: "isn't the C++ pattern the test-peer you mentioned earlier. Why wouldn't we do that?", then "Do (b) then."
Record: this register; docs/plans/test-scaffolding-consolidation.md for the first step. Marked: mine. [dec-A81]

**Joint structure-and-value moves join the mixing research**
The non-conjugate move strategy of the founding engine design, joint proposals of tree structure and leaf values accepted on the likelihood ratio, with a second sweep variant that evaluates the likelihood at total fits, goes on the post-release mixing research list as a candidate mixing improvement for every leaf model, not only as the route for Gaussian-process leaves under a likelihood with no working-Gaussian form. No shipped family needs it for correctness: Gaussian-process leaves fit under the Gaussian, probit, logistic, negative binomial, ordinal and Student-t families through their conditionally Gaussian forms, the multinomial family refusing them because it runs one forest per category. The agents had left it designed but unscheduled until a consumer asked, never putting it to the maintainer; building it before 1.0-0 was also weighed. The maintainer, 2026-09-27: "I think it should be in (b), in case it improves mixing. I don't know that it needs to be just for GPs in that case."
Record: this register; docs/design/gp-leaves.md part 2; TODO tree-mixing-proposals. Marked: mine. [dec-A71]

**Three recorded additions each take their own course**
Of three additive items a pre-release review had designed and left unscheduled, each takes its own course. A plain-C getter for each chain's current sigma joins the shipped header in the header change the host-neutral engine work already brings (dec-B85), so compiled consumers rebuild once; a consumer reads sigma per saved draw through the draw callback meanwhile. Serializing the internal data handle, the preprocessed predictors built once and shared across samplers, is dropped: it is unexported, nothing saves it, and a non-R host such as a Python binding would pickle its raw arrays and rebuild the handle on load, cheap next to fitting; pickling a fitted sampler from such a host is the opaque C state buffer already conditional under dec-A29. Variable-selection inference by Bleich et al.'s permutation thresholds is R over bart() and extract, with a plot, and goes into TODO as post-release work gated on first showing it is a good idea and used, against the DART prior the package ships and later selection work; the maintainer: "we don't need to cram everyone's idea into the package." The agents had left all three recorded and unscheduled, never putting them to the maintainer, and building the first two into the host-neutral work was also weighed. The maintainer, 2026-09-27: "Sure sounds good."
Record: this register; docs/plans/prerc-surface-freeze.md; TODO host-neutral engine and variable-selection-permutation. Marked: mine. [dec-A73]

**The engine's printing is chosen when the package is built**
Where the engine's progress output, messages and warnings go is fixed when the package is built, as the math is: R's console under R, standard output for a standalone build such as a prebuilt library, and whatever a future binding supplies in its own build, Python's output stream for instance so that notebooks show it. The support library's run-time print pointers, which every host installs and nothing calls, are removed with the rest of that work. The alternative was run-time hooks a host installs, which cost nothing in speed, since printing is not on any hot path, but leave a null pointer that crashes if a host forgets to install it, and which no current or planned host needs, since every host compiles the engine into its own build. The cost falls on a later host that loads a prebuilt library and wants output routed through its own streams rather than standard output, Julia being the likely one: it would need a run-time override added to the C interface then, an addition that breaks no one. Nothing is visible to an R user. The maintainer asked why printing should not be chosen at build time too, then left the decision to the agents, 2026-09-24: "I guess I don't mind eventually supporting Julia. I leave the decision up to you." See also: [dec-B85], [dec-C04]. The maintainer, 2026-09-27, marking it: "(a) for now. Revisit if an application ever appears."; a host that needs run-time routing reopens it.
Record: this register. Marked: mine. [dec-A74]

**Grouped random effects leave the package**
The package no longer fits BART with grouped random intercepts; that model now lives in stan4bart. rbart_vi and its six S3 methods stay until 1.1-0 as tombstones: calling rbart_vi, or predict, extract, fitted, residuals or plot on an old rbart fit, stops with an error naming stan4bart and saying its group-spread prior differs, so results move, and printing an old fit shows its call with the same note. No alternative was weighed when it was removed; the later ruling considered an R-only grouped intercept driven through the sampler's offset each sweep and rejected it. A user of 0.9-x who called rbart_vi has no runnable path in the package, and the released bartCause's grouped fits stop with that error until bartCause routes them through stan4bart. The check on the group standard deviation's mixing that was meant to gate the removal failed on two of three seeds while the removal was kept; the maintainer then made stan4bart's own mixing the condition for shipping, and stan4bart has since met it. See also: [dec-B105]. The maintainer marked it theirs on 2026-09-27: "Yes, that's right." For 1.0-0, rbart_vi returns deprecated as a port of 0.9-34's R implementation instead of a tombstone; see [dec-B130].
Record: docs/design/retire-grouped-random-effects.md, which quotes no maintainer words on the removal. Marked: mine. [dec-A01]

**How a removed function reaches users**
