The cost is that the default prior depends on the data and, for a 0/1 treatment written as a number, on the treated share, its default effect 2, 2.5 and 4.6 times the factor's at 50, 20 and 5 percent treated though the fits match the factor's; that a single wild value inflates the scale and the normal coefficient cannot recover (one 1000 among 500 rows: 0.34, 0.36, where the half-Cauchy gave 0.21, 0.81), the printed scale being the only sign; that a stated sd in the wrong units is still a zero estimate; and that a column far from zero still fits badly until the caller centres it. The maintainer on 2026-10-06: "Scale the default and keep normal coefficient". See also: [dec-B257], [dec-B258], [dec-B253], [dec-B260].
Record: this register; TODO forest-prior-args. Marked: blank. [dec-B261]

**A caller may opt a drawn coefficient into a heavy-tailed law; built after the merge to main**
The law of a forest's drawn coefficient is not a caller's argument under dec-B253 and the default for a multiplied forest is normal (dec-B261). The maintainer, told that, asked "are these user-settable? Can they opt-into heavy tailed distributions?" The opt-in is one argument on forest(), its spelling still to be decided, under which the coefficient's size is half-Cauchy with the same prior median; the default does not change. Its user is a caller who runs the sampler inside a larger one and redraws the multiplier, whose scale drifts during the run: measured on 2026-10-06, a column replaced by one 30 times smaller under a normal coefficient gave slope error 0.27 with a 90 percent interval covering 0.50 of the time, against 0.18 and 0.88 under the half-Cauchy, and a stated sd 100 times too small 0.40 and 0.17 against 0.17 and 0.90. The alternative was leaving the law fixed, as bcf does. The cost is one more argument and about 300 lines with the reader's label and an exact test. The maintainer on 2026-10-06: "Sure, opt-in for 1.0. Post main-merge." See also: [dec-B261], [dec-B253].
Record: TODO coefficient-law-opt-in. Marked: blank. [dec-B262]

**A 0/1 treatment written as a number is scaled as any number, and the help says so**
Under dec-B261 a numeric column's default sd is per standard deviation of the column, and a 0/1 column's standard deviation follows the treated share (0.5 at half treated, 0.4 at 20 percent, 0.22 at 5 percent), so the default prior on the treatment effect of a 0/1 number is 2, 2.5 and 4.6 times the one factor(z) gets. No exception is made: a number is a number (dec-B260), the help for forest says that a treatment belongs in a factor or a logical, where sd is the treatment effect, and gives the figures, and the fit prints the default it resolved. Measured on 2026-10-06 at those three shares, the number against the factor: effect error 0.282, 0.332, 0.449 against 0.293, 0.335, 0.449; a 90 percent interval covering 0.83 to 0.85 against 0.76 to 0.80; with no effect, estimates of size 0.072, 0.102, 0.195 against 0.072, 0.093, 0.181; and the factor mixing two to six times slower. rstanarm since 2020, VCBART and flexBART scale a binary column by its standard deviation as any other; arm uses the gap between the two values, a rule rstanarm had and removed. The alternatives were that gap for a two-valued column, which decides by content and under which one stray third value moved the prior 2 to 7 times, and two standard deviations for every column, which matches the factor at half treated only, which no package uses without the two-valued case, and which covered worse under probit (0.83 against 0.87). The cost is that a user who writes a treatment as a 0/1 number and does not read the printed prior has a wider prior on the effect than bcf's convention, more so the rarer the treatment. The maintainer on 2026-10-06: "Leave it and document it." See also: [dec-B261], [dec-B260], [dec-B258].
Record: TODO forest-prior-args. Marked: blank. [dec-B263]

**A numeric multiplier far from zero is the caller's to centre: no warning and no centring inside the package**
In y = f1(x) + w f2(x) the first forest is the response at w = 0, so a column that never comes near zero, an age or a year, makes that forest an extrapolation and the term w f2 mostly a second copy of it. Measured on 2026-10-06, slope error and the coverage of a 90 percent interval: 0.17 and 0.90 centred; 0.22 and 0.66 at 3 standard deviations from zero; 0.35 and 0.22 at 10; under probit 0.30 and 0.54 at 3, with no sign from the chain. The package does nothing to the column and raises nothing: the help for a numeric multiplier leads with the centred form, forest(basis = ~ scale(w)) or I(w - c), for a column that is an ordinary covariate, says why in a sentence (the first forest's default is placed on the observed response, and uncentred it must describe the response at zero), and the fit prints the column's mean and standard deviation beside the default it resolved; scale() in a basis predicts from the training rows (dec-B257). The orchestrator had recommended a warning when zero lies outside the column's range; the maintainer asked "Is the warning disproportionate? Should those users be centering, just for statistical purposes? Should try and silently fit a (roughly) equivalent model for them?" A warning suits a call that is probably a mistake and these are ordinary inputs, so it was withdrawn. Centring is the right thing statistically, the same model under the prior the defaults are built for where an unmultiplied forest covers the same columns; rstanarm and brms centre linear terms inside and report the intercept in the original terms, VCBART and flexBART centre. The package does not, as lme4 does not centre a random slope's predictor nor mgcv a numeric by: a default centre changes what a stated prior on the first forest means, from the response at zero to the response at the mean, for a dose or a treatment where zero means none; it cannot be applied only where zero is outside the data without deciding by content (dec-B260); a caller who redraws the multiplier would get a centre taken from starting values; and all eight panel voices called centring the user's job. Asked why the recommendation was "for 1.0" only, the orchestrator withdrew the qualifier: a default cannot change after a release without changing fits, so this is the design and anything later is an addition a caller asks for. The cost is that a user who multiplies by an uncentred covariate and reads neither the help nor the printed prior gets a poor slope with nothing to flag it. The maintainer on 2026-10-06: "OK, then use your recommendation." See also: [dec-B261], [dec-B257].
Record: TODO forest-prior-args. Marked: blank. [dec-B264]

**A replaced multiplier keeps its derived default sd unless updateBasisScale asks for a new one**
The default sd of a forest that multiplies a numeric column is derived from the column's standard deviation when the sampler is created and kept with the data (dec-B261). When the column is replaced through setForestBasis the default is kept, as setPredictor keeps the split points and setResponse the response scale (dec-B231: derived data changes only when the caller is explicit), and an argument, updateBasisScale, FALSE by default, re-derives it from the new column when TRUE. The update restates that forest's prior for the next sweep and leaves its trees as they are, as setLeafPrior does, a new column being no rescaling of the old one; like updateScale it is for burn-in only, a prior that moves afterwards making draws across sweeps no longer comparable. TRUE on a forest whose sd was stated is refused by name, there being no default to update; a constant column is refused wherever a default would have to be derived from it, at creation with no stated sd and on an update; and setData follows dec-B231, deriving when the new data object carries no value, keeping one it carries, an explicit updateBasisScale overriding both. The name is its own because updateScale on setData already means the response range: one argument per part of the sampler, as updateCutPoints, updateScale and updateStandardization (dec-B233). The orchestrator had recommended keeping the default with no argument, the caller restating sd through setLeafPrior, and the design's critic refusing the first replacement until sd was stated; the maintainer asked "Should this be treated like the response scale, where users can have it update during burn-in and be fixed after, using a flag?", which is the package's own idiom and answers what keeping alone left open, a caller whose starting column is arbitrary. The other alternative, recomputing on every replacement, is what the branch did: measured, the coefficient's prior sd moved from 0.77 to 0.25 between a column and three times it inside one chain, and every panel voice asked ruled it out. The cost is one more argument, and that a caller who never states sd and never updates samples under a default set by the first column: a column that later shrinks 30-fold gave slope error 0.27 and a 90 percent interval covering 0.50 of the time, against 0.18 and 0.88 at the right scale. No other package lets a multiplier be replaced during a run. The maintainer on 2026-10-06, asked to confirm the rule and the name: "Yes and updateBasisScale." See also: [dec-B261], [dec-B231], [dec-B233], [dec-B254].
Record: TODO forest-prior-args. Marked: blank. [dec-B265]

**A forest states its sd as a plain argument, forest(sd = ), with leaf.prior = normal(sd = ) accepted as the long form**
dec-B246 gave forest() the front door's prior arguments, so a forest's spread was written forest(x1 + x2, basis = ~ dose, leaf.prior = normal(sd = 30)). Shown full calls in that form, with a several-column basis and one sd per column, the maintainer said: "Those are incredibly hard to parse. Can you design a cleaner form that places the sd closer to its basis? If we need to workshop this, maybe we should." A workshop on 2026-10-06 put four notations for the same five models to eight readers who saw only the calls and to four design voices: the form as designed; a plain sd inside the term; mgcv's forest(x, by = dose, sd = 30); and priors kept apart from the formula and matched by label. All four voices chose a plain sd inside forest(), in the response's units, with the long form still accepted so that a prior read from a sampler can be handed back and a later law has a place; the readers found no notation clearly easier, every one asked what the sd was the sd of, and none could read cgm(3, 0.25) without its argument names. The rule: forest(sd = 30) is the spelling the help shows; it is the scale of that forest's leaf prior, the quantity leaf.prior = normal(sd = 30) states, so the two are one statement, the maintainer having asked "Is it part of the leaf.prior?"; forest() accepts either and refuses both together by name; the reader and the writer on a live sampler trade normal() objects as dec-B246 ruled, in one unit. In a model of several forests the only leaf prior a forest can take is a normal with an sd, so the wrapper carried one number. The alternative was dec-B246 as it stood, one spelling and longer. The cost is two spellings of one statement, and that anything beyond the one number, a coefficient held or heavy-tailed among them, has to go in the long form. Examples name cgm's arguments, and the fit prints each sd both per unit and per standard deviation of its column, the slip every voice named being a stated sd left unchanged when its column is rescaled. The maintainer on 2026-10-06: "OK, option A is fine." Revises dec-B246 in part. See also: [dec-B246], [dec-B253], [dec-B261].
Record: TODO forest-prior-args. Marked: blank. [dec-B266]

**A multiplied forest is written one way, forest(x, basis = ~ w); the colon form goes**
A formula could state a forest's multiplier two ways: w:forest(x1 + x2), which takes only a bare name or factor(name) and sends anything else to the other form by its refusal, and forest(x1 + x2, basis = ~ w), which takes any expression and is also how the list interface writes it. The colon form is removed: a multiplier is the forest's basis argument, in a formula and in a list alike, and the colon is refused with a message naming the form to use. In the workshop of 2026-10-06 both readers shown a call that used the two spellings side by side stumbled on there being two, four of the six readers who saw the colon read it as an interaction, as it is in lm, and of the six who were not shown a multiplier inside the call five proposed one unprompted; the design voices split two against two, the two for the colon liking that it reads as z times tau(x). The argument keeps the package's word, basis, which setForestBasis already uses, over mgcv's by, under which a factor promises one function per level, which this model is not. The alternative was keeping both. The cost is the shorter spelling for the commonest case, and that forest(), new in 1.0-0, has its tests, help and examples respelled before the release; no released call changes. The maintainer on 2026-10-06: "Drop the colon. I also like z times tua(x), but colons don't directly read as that." See also: [dec-B266], [dec-B260].
Record: TODO forest-prior-args. Marked: blank. [dec-B267]

**The first forest of a formula may be written as forest(x1 + x2)**
In a fitting function's formula the plain terms are the first forest, and y ~ forest(x1 + x2) + forest(x1 + x2, basis = ~ dose) is refused ("a formula whose only right-hand-side content is a forest() term has no predictors left for the base forest"). That form is now accepted: a forest() term with no basis is a forest with no multiplier, so each forest() in a formula is one function, and the multi-forest examples in the help are written that way. y ~ x1 + x2 + forest(x1 + x2, basis = ~ dose) keeps working, the plain terms being the first forest as they are for a single-forest fit. In the workshop of 2026-10-06, five of the eight readers shown only the calls took the plain x1 + x2 beside a forest() term for linear terms, the worst misreading found, in every notation tried, and three of them proposed writing the first forest out. The alternative was leaving the refusal. The cost is a second way to write the first forest; what a formula holding both plain terms and a forest() with no basis means is for the design to settle. The maintainer on 2026-10-06: "Option A." See also: [dec-B267], [dec-B241].
Record: TODO forest-prior-args. Marked: blank. [dec-B268]

**A forest states one sd in 1.0; a longer sd is refused, and an unnamed vector for good**
A forest multiplied by several columns, one shared function times a weighted sum of them, has a default per column, each by its own standard deviation (dec-B261), and takes one stated sd, that many response units per unit of every column. In 1.0-0 sd is one unnamed number: an sd of length greater than one is refused by name, named or not, and a name on a single number is refused too, so that stating a size per column can be added later without changing what any 1.0 call means; an unnamed vector stays refused then, since reordering the columns would reassign its numbers with no sign. The form of the later per-column statement is not settled: a vector named by column, sd = c(dose = 30, age = 0.05), which all four of the second workshop round's voices chose over a positional one, or a wrapper on the basis term, which the maintainer is considering ("I still dislike how basis and sd are separated"). What the release fixes now for either: how a basis's columns are named, and a reader, extract and print that are per column, the default already resolving to one number per column. In that round, on 2026-10-06, all six readers shown only the calls read a two-column basis as two separate functions, whatever the form of the sd; naming the vector made them surer which number went with which column (70 and 75 percent against 65 and 70) and no surer what the model was. The alternative was accepting the named vector in 1.0, which two voices preferred because a reader that returns a default forest's two numbers should be able to hand them back; the reader instead returns what was stated, the default as the default, and shows the resolved numbers beside it. The cost is that a caller with columns in different units who wants to state sizes scales the columns in the basis, which is the same model and reads badly. The maintainer on 2026-10-06: "Option A is fine". See also: [dec-B261], [dec-B266].
Record: TODO forest-prior-args. Marked: blank. [dec-B269]

**A basis is written as the right-hand side of a model formula, with no tilde**
A forest's basis was a one-sided formula whose right-hand side was evaluated as R code, so basis = ~ dose + age was one column, the sum, two columns needed cbind(dose, age), and the package carried its own code to rebuild scale() and poly() at new rows as lm would (dec-B257, dec-A162). The basis is now the right-hand side of a model formula, written without the tilde, as a forest's covariates already are: forest(x1 + x2, basis = dose + age) is two columns, I(dose + age) their sum, factor(z) one column per level, poly(dose, 2) and scale(age) what they are in lm, at predict too, through R's own model frame. forest() takes the argument unevaluated and finds its names in the data and then where the call was made, as lm does. The maintainer on 2026-10-06, of the cbind form: "I also dislike cbind - what else would a `+` mean in that context?", and of the tilde: "will anything ever be on the left-hand-side of the `~`? If not, we should do some formula / language hacking so we can interpret whatever is given as the RHS of a formula." Nothing is planned for the left-hand side. In the workshop's second round all four voices had asked that + at the top of a basis be refused, a sum fitted in silence being the worst reading, so as to leave this meaning open; it is taken directly instead. Made by the orchestrator and not yet ruled on: a one-sided formula is still accepted, for a caller who builds the basis in a variable; and what a basis's intercept, an interaction of two basis terms and a basis mixing a factor with numbers mean is for the design. The cost is that a basis written for the old reading with + changes meaning, none having been released, and that forest() evaluates one of its arguments in the caller's data, as subset() and the fitting functions do. See also: [dec-B267], [dec-B268], [dec-B260], [dec-B257], [dec-A162].
Record: TODO forest-prior-args. Marked: blank. [dec-B270]

**A forest's coefficient law is one argument, amplitude; fixed() holds the coefficient, and update.amplitude goes**
A forest's coefficient was held with forest(update.amplitude = FALSE), and the opt-in heavy-tailed law of dec-B262 was to be a further argument whose spelling was undecided. The two are one statement, the law of the forest's coefficient, and forest() takes it as one argument: amplitude = fixed() holds the coefficient at the value its shape has (dec-B253), nothing stated is the normal law, drawn, as now, and the heavy-tailed law is added after the merge to main as another value of the same argument, amplitude = student(3) in the form shown to the maintainer. It is stated once for a forest and covers all of that forest's coefficients, so a basis's terms stay bare; update.amplitude is removed before the release, since afterwards it would stay as a second spelling, and bartCause's bcf, which passes it, changes one line. The maintainer had said of a wrapper on each basis term, normal(dose, sd = 30), "you have to put `normal` everywhere", and then "if student distributions are planned we'll need a way to differentiate"; this differentiates without a wrapper. All four voices of the workshop's second round asked that the hold and the heavy tail be two values of one argument and that update.amplitude be folded in before 1.0-0; they put it inside the prior object, leaf.prior = normal(sd = , amplitude = ), where the orchestrator proposed a plain argument beside sd, as dec-B266 made sd. fixed() is the constructor the package already uses for a value that is not drawn. The alternative was keeping update.amplitude and adding a second argument for the heavy tail. The cost is a rename of an argument new in 1.0-0, with its tests and help. The maintainer on 2026-10-06, shown the two options: "I like fixed()". See also: [dec-B262], [dec-B253], [dec-B254], [dec-B266].
Record: TODO forest-prior-args; TODO coefficient-law-opt-in. Marked: blank. [dec-B271]

**How a size per column is stated is decided after 1.0, on condition that it can be added without a breaking change**
Three ways of stating a different size for each column of a several-column basis are on the table and none is chosen: a vector named by column, sd = c(dose = 30, age = 0.05); a wrapper on the basis term, normal(dose, sd = 30) + normal(age, sd = 0.05), of which the maintainer said "you have to put `normal` everywhere"; and a unit on the term, basis = dose / 30 + age / 10, which the maintainer floated. A reading test on 2026-10-06, eight readers shown only the calls and asked what one more unit of dose does to y: both readers of the named vector gave the right figures, one of the two shown the wrapper did with a guess, and none of the four shown the division did outright, taking it for a rescaling of the data, which three of them said they would do before the call. The choice waits until after the release, the maintainer's condition being that it must not then need a breaking change: "It can wait as long as we're confident that we're not likely to have to introduce a breaking change to support it." What 1.0-0 does so that each of the three is an addition, changing the meaning of no call it accepts: an sd of length greater than one and a name on a single sd are refused by name (dec-B269); a basis term that calls a prior constructor is refused by name; a term divided or multiplied by a number at the top of a basis is refused by name, as R's own formula code refuses it today ("invalid model formula"), while I(dose / 30) is accepted as the rescaled column it is and would mean the same under the third form; the names of a basis's columns are fixed, as coef() would show them; one sd on several columns means that many response units per unit of each, now and later; and the reader, extract and print are per column from the start. The engine holds one scale per column from the slice that builds the default of dec-B261, which lands before the release and must keep it; the tip does not yet, the design's prototype does. With that in place no stored state or compiled interface changes with any of the three. What would break a 1.0 call is changing what one sd on several columns means, or how columns are named; nothing else was found, and the critic of the surface design is asked to try to build a counterexample. The alternative was choosing now. See also: [dec-B269], [dec-B270], [dec-B271], [dec-B266].
Record: TODO forest-prior-args. Marked: blank. [dec-B272]

**A star in a basis is refused in 1.0**
With a basis read as the right-hand side of a model formula (dec-B270), basis = dose * age would be lm's three columns, dose, age and their product, where the same text on the tip is one column, the product, so the reading would change with no sign; and a star by a number is one of the spellings a later unit on the term would use (dec-B272). In 1.0-0 a star at the top of a basis is refused by name; the product is written I(dose * age) and the three columns dose + age + I(dose * age). The alternative was lm's meaning. The cost is that a caller who expects a model formula's crossing writes it out. The design's critic, asked on 2026-10-06 what in the accepted grammar could force a breaking change later, named this as the one operator to settle now. The maintainer on 2026-10-06: "Refuse * for now." See also: [dec-B270], [dec-B272].
Record: TODO forest-prior-args. Marked: blank. [dec-B273]

**A forest's default tree count and tree prior go by its kind, not by where it is written**
The first forest of a model takes the fit's own tree count and tree prior, and a later one the multiplied forest's defaults, fewer and shallower trees, so in a formula whose forests all have a basis, y ~ forest(x1, basis = a) + forest(x2, basis = b), whichever is written first is treated as the plain one and swapping the two terms is another model (run by the design's critic on 2026-10-06: 20 trees of 4.0 nodes each against 50 of 1.5). The defaults now go by kind, as a forest's default sd already does (dec-B258, dec-B261): a forest with no basis takes the usual defaults and the fitting function's n.trees and tree.prior are its; a forest with a basis takes the multiplied forest's defaults wherever it is written, in a formula and in a list alike. Where no forest is without a basis, a tree count or tree prior given to the fitting function has no forest to belong to, 100 each and 100 in all being equally likely readings, and that argument alone is refused by name, with a message saying to state it on a forest; the model is accepted. The orchestrator and the critic had recommended refusing such a formula in 1.0; the maintainer: "I think A would confuse users since it would be an unexpected failure of an input that seems like it should work. What is going to match a users intent here?" What matches it is that two forests of one kind are treated alike and that addition commutes. The other alternative was written order, as built. The cost is that a fit which puts a multiplied forest first and states no tree count or tree prior changes its defaults, no such call having been released; that the rule of dec-B241, the first forest's count being the fit's, becomes the plain forest's; and that it is not built. bartCause's bcf is not affected, its first forest having no basis. The maintainer on 2026-10-06: "Use B then." See also: [dec-B241], [dec-B246], [dec-B268].
Record: TODO forest-prior-args. Marked: blank. [dec-B274]

**extract(type = "leaf.prior.sd") returns a list by forest of vectors named by column**
For a model of several forests, extract(fit, type = "leaf.prior.sd") returns a list with one entry for each forest, named by the forest's label, each entry a numeric vector with one number for each column of that forest's basis, named as the column is, and a single unnamed number for a forest with no basis; the numbers are the sd in force, stated or resolved from the default, in the response's units per unit of the column. A default already resolves to a different number for each column (dec-B261), so one number for a forest could not describe a several-column basis, and the shape is fixed for good, since a size stated per column later (dec-B272) has to come back in the same form. The alternative was one flat named vector with names joining the forest and the column, dose.dose and dose.age, which puts every number in one vector and makes a caller split names to find a forest's own; the orchestrator's first proposal, before the default was per column, had been that flat vector. The cost is that the result for an ordinary fit of one-column forests is a list of length-one vectors. The maintainer on 2026-10-06, shown the two: "A." See also: [dec-B269], [dec-B272], [dec-B253].
Record: TODO forest-prior-args. Marked: blank. [dec-B275]

**Selecting a forest by its label is built before the merge to main; updateBasisScale and the long form on forest() after it**
Three pieces of the forest surface are additions that nothing else waits on, about 2400 of the 21300 lines planned for the arc: setForestBasis(updateBasisScale = TRUE), which re-derives a default sd during burn-in (dec-B265); the long form leaf.prior = normal(sd = ) on forest() (dec-B266); and selecting a forest by its label, as getLeafPrior("dose"). Selecting by label is built before the merge to main: the reader, extract and print already name forests by label (dec-B275), and a forest that can be named and not asked for would be odd. The other two are built after the merge to main, for 1.0-0 still, as the heavy-tailed coefficient law is (dec-B262): until then a caller who replaces a column whose forest is at the default sd states the sd, and a caller writes sd = and not the long form, which is refused as R refuses an argument a function does not have, so that accepting it later changes no call. Run by the design's critic on 2026-10-06: leaving any of the three out breaks nothing later. The alternatives were building all three before the merge and deferring all three. The cost is that a prior read from a sampler, a normal() object, cannot be handed to forest() until the long form lands, only to setLeafPrior; and that a default-sd forest whose column is replaced keeps the first column's default with no way to refresh it but stating one. The maintainer on 2026-10-06, of keeping the label and deferring the other two: "Sure, option C. For deferring, just until it lands on main." See also: [dec-B262], [dec-B265], [dec-B266], [dec-B272], [dec-B275].
Record: TODO forest-prior-args; TODO forest-after-merge. Marked: blank. [dec-B276]

**A saved state names its zero-weight rows, so a Student-t restore under other weights redraws only the rows that enter**
A Student-t fit holds a latent scale for every row, and a row at weight zero keeps one drawn without that row's residual. A state stores the scales and a digest of the weights it was stored under, not which rows were at zero, so a state installed under other weights, by setState, a copy or a reload, could not tell a stale scale from a live one and redrew the scale of every row in the likelihood (dec-A160); the exact route was to put the stored weights back first and restore then, an order the manual gave. The state now also records which rows were at weight zero when it was stored, and an install under other weights redraws the scale of exactly the rows that enter the likelihood, those at zero then and positive now, as the same setWeights call would; an install under its own weights draws nothing, as before, and a state stored before this, which names no rows, is installed as it was, every scale in the likelihood redrawn where it carries a digest and none where it does not. The orchestrator had recommended leaving it, the case being rare and an exact order documented; the maintainer: "Option B. A sounds hostile to the user." The alternative was that. The cost is about 480 lines by the plan's estimate, tests included, and a state wider by one byte for each row of every Student-t fit, weighted or not, since an unweighted fit's state installed under weights with zeros had every scale redrawn too; not built. With setState first and the old weights second the sampler is right in law, and the stored chain bit for bit only when no row went to zero and back; with the weights put back first it is bit for bit always. The maintainer on 2026-10-06, as quoted. Revises dec-A160 in part. See also: [dec-A160], [dec-B238], [dec-B234].
Record: docs/plans/state-zero-weight-rows.md. Marked: blank. [dec-B277]

**A monotone sampler resets a tree only on a forced update; an unforced one that would break a tree is refused**
New predictor values can leave a monotone tree's leaf values out of order along the constrained predictor: a forced update empties leaves and merges them beside new neighbours, and a column that first gains missing values gains a position that relates leaves the order did not. As built (dec-A129, dec-A131) such a tree had every leaf set to zero, drawing nothing, on both routes, so an unforced setPredictor that brought a column its first missing value was accepted and reset the tree, the one unforced update that changed a fit it did not refuse; an unforced update that would empty a leaf was already refused. The maintainer was given the reset, a refusal on a monotone sampler, and the reset with a warning, for the forced update: "forceUpdate is supposed to \"figure it out\", and is decent during burn-in. Without it, it should reject if it would leave the sampler in an incoherent state, as it does today." So a forced update keeps the silent reset, as do setData, setCutPoints and a warm start, which take no such flag and always complete (the orchestrator's reading), and an unforced update, whole-matrix, by column or row by row, that would leave a tree out of order is refused as one that empties a leaf is, the sampler left as it was; the help says both. In 24 trial forced updates on 400 rows, a free column redrawn for a quarter or all of the rows or flattened, at 10, 50 and 200 trees, no tree was reset. The unforced refusal is not built; about 450 lines by its plan. The plan found the one such update narrower than "a column": only an unordered factor column's first missing value relates leaves anew, a numeric or ordered column's going left at every rule (no pair added in 20000 random trees), and from R it arrives through the whole-matrix form given codes and the joint row form, the column forms refusing a factor's first missing value by name already; a setState that merges emptied leaves resets as the forced calls do. The maintainer on 2026-10-06, as quoted. Revises dec-A129 and dec-A131 in part. See also: [dec-A129], [dec-A131].
Record: docs/plans/monotone-unforced-refusal.md. Marked: blank. [dec-B278]

**The joint row-by-row update is fixed for a factor before the merge to main**
Planning dec-B278 turned up a defect outside it: updatePredictorPerObservationJointly reads its values as the engine's category codes, from 0, and a factor handed to it arrives as R's codes, from 1, so every level is installed as the next one with no message (200 of 200 rows in the probe), or the call is refused as naming no existing category when the top level is present. The manual says the values are numeric there, so a factor is undocumented input that nothing refuses. Told of it, the maintainer: "Let's fix the updatePredictorPerObservationJointly bug pre-merge." No alternative was put. What the fix does with a factor, match its labels to the column's levels as setPredictor by column does or refuse it by name, is left to the slice's plan. The maintainer on 2026-10-06, as quoted.
Record: TODO factor-column-update-forms. Marked: blank. [dec-B279]

## C. Agent-made decisions with no identified cost

**C entry points register under full names**
The compiled entry points are registered under their full symbol names rather than the unprefixed method names the released package used. No alternative was weighed. Nothing is visible to an R user; the naming is what makes an old compiled consumer fail at lookup instead of resolving a same-named symbol with a different signature. Not yet ruled on.
Record: docs/design/public-surface.md, which calls handle-prefixed names defensible. Marked: not mine. [dec-C01]

**The compiled interface is checked at build**
The package asserts its own side of the compiled interface when it builds: a signature token, a recomputed hash, and the offsets, sizes and alignments of the three structs the interface exposes. No alternative was weighed. Nothing is visible to a user; drift inside the package is caught at compile time. Not yet ruled on.
Record: code only, in the flat C interface implementation. Marked: not mine. [dec-C02]

**Internal call registrations get a new prefix**
The registrations R uses to reach compiled code are renamed to an engine-specific prefix, 58 of 65, with seven utility entries keeping the plain dbarts prefix and the dynamic-library line unchanged. No alternative was weighed. Nothing is visible to a user, and no R-visible name changes. Not yet ruled on.
Record: code only, in the registration table. Marked: not mine. [dec-C03]

**The support library no longer needs R**
The support library is host-agnostic: it carries no standard input-output and no R symbol, its printing declared as function pointers the host installs. No alternative was weighed when this was done; run-time hooks against a choice made when the package is built were weighed later, under dec-A74. Nothing is visible to a user. Nothing calls those pointers, and after the merge they are removed, printing and math for the whole engine being chosen when the package is built. See also: [dec-A74], [dec-B85].
Record: code only, in the support library's input-output unit. Marked: blank. [dec-C04]

**The grown warm start stays opt-in**
A sampler starts from a forest drawn from the prior, and the warm start that grows trees from the root first is available through an argument. The alternative, defaulting that warm start on, was measured against a criterion fixed in advance and killed, the measurement showing a cost in noise-heavy and large-sample designs. A user who wants it asks for it by name, and the manual says which families refuse the option. The maintainer was given three options and took this one. See also: [dec-B94].
Record: docs/design/grow-from-root-default.md, whose status records the measured kill. Marked: superseded by dec-B94. [dec-C05]

**Two research switches ship unbuilt**
Two compile-time switches meant for research measurements ship in the source and are defined by no shipped build. No alternative was weighed. Nothing is visible to a user; both say in the code that they are for private builds only. Not yet ruled on.
Record: code only, in the moves header and the C++ test makefile. Marked: not mine. [dec-C06]

**Two developer tools ship unwired**
The stale-install detector and the snapshot regeneration script ship as local developer tools, wired into no workflow. No alternative was weighed. Nothing is visible to a user; both say so in their own headers. Not yet ruled on.
Record: code only, in the tools directory. Marked: not mine. [dec-C07]

**Two header calls: scope and a parameter name**
Two open questions about the header's shape were settled: the first cleanup item covers seven entries rather than the five originally named, and the basis setter's parameter is renamed to say that the data is row-major instead of transposing the contract to match the old name. The alternatives were the narrower five-entry scope and the transposition. A caller who lays the data out the other way still gets no error, only a parameter name that says which way round it goes. A later ruling took both entries this shaped out of the header. No maintainer ruling on the two choices themselves is on record. See also: [dec-B86].
Record: docs/plans/capi-shape.md, which lists both as open decisions for the maintainer and records the outcome with no attribution. Marked: not mine; superseded by dec-B86, which took both entries it shaped out of the header. [dec-B61]

**Engine test accessors split by body size**
Behind the test peer, the engine's test-only accessors with one-line bodies became static functions on the peer and the four with real bodies became private members; the ForTesting suffix is gone, cross-class forwarders live on the peer, the column store's two reads go straight through its existing public test-set block, and the sampler's per-chain navigation stays public. The alternatives were private members everywhere with peer forwarders, or every body moved into the test header. Nothing is visible to a user. Not yet ruled on. See also: [dec-A81].
Record: docs/plans/test-scaffolding-consolidation.md. Marked: blank. [dec-C08]

**Constructor hints name only the list the argument reads**
An unfound prior, family or forest constructor gets a hint naming the constructor list the argument being read takes constructors from, the family and prior lists for the family argument, inside which a residual prior is written, from one shared table, and no hint where no list applies; the hint carries no NEWS entry, as the forest hint had none. The alternative was a hint searching every list, which pointed tree.prior = student(3) at the family list. Nothing breaks. Not yet ruled on. See also: [dec-A75].
Record: docs/plans/constructor-vocabulary.md. Marked: blank. [dec-C09]

**xbart's parallel test uses a built-in generator**
xbart's parity across thread counts under a non-default generator is tested with Knuth-TAOCP-2002, no user-supplied generator being loadable portably in a test; the gaussian equivalence baseline of the time, since superseded, was re-recorded for its two xbart scenarios, with a hand-rebuilt cross-validation cell as the oracle, and a test that tree counts in one unit draw on separate streams was added. The alternative was no parity test for non-default kinds. Nothing is visible to a user. Not yet ruled on. See also: [dec-A90].
Record: benchmarks/baselines/MANIFEST. Marked: blank. [dec-C10]

**k = chi() resolves through a wrapper's dots**
A k = chi(...) that a wrapper forwards to bart through its dots resolves in the prior vocabulary, as when written directly and as the family and prior arguments already did, which makes true for k the help's statement that constructors resolve inside a wrapper's dots. The alternative was narrowing the help to say that k = chi() must be written in the direct call or as dbartsPriors$chi. Nothing breaks; a call that failed with "could not find function" fits, and the same call to 0.9-34's bart2 failed that way too. Not yet ruled on.
Record: commit 0f5ba74b. Marked: blank. [dec-C11]

**Which warnings carry a class at the edges of the ruling**
Applying dec-B161, the agents left sparseFactor's warnings plain, since they repeat base R's factor warnings (an invalid level giving NA, a replacement length that is not a multiple, an operator not meaningful for factors), which base R leaves unclassed; and classed rbart_vi's fallbacks as dbartsFallbackWarning: verbose output dropped under several threads, a multithreaded run falling back to one thread, group.by recycled for a test set without group.by.test, and random effects drawn from their distribution for test groups training did not see. The alternatives were classing sparseFactor's warnings as fallbacks and leaving rbart_vi's plain. Nothing breaks. Not yet ruled on. See also: [dec-B161].
Record: commits 120644f4 and 6a7067b6. Marked: blank. [dec-C12]

**Argument forwarding reads the formals of the function the call matched**
redirectCall, which re-targets a front door's matched call at the function it forwards to, reads the formals of the function the call matched (its caller by default, or those the caller passes) instead of evaluating the call's head, so a front door called through an alias (fn <- bart; fn(...)) forwards its arguments as when called by name; it had found its own formal fn and sent every bart argument to dbartsControl. The alternative, inferred when this entry was written and not weighed at the time, was evaluating the head in another frame, where an alias may name nothing or the wrong function. The fix also repairs 0.9-34's bart2, which fails when called through an alias made inside a function; nothing else is visible. Not yet ruled on.
Record: commit d6389c28. Marked: blank. [dec-C13]

**A factor basis may leave a level empty on setForestBasis**
setForestBasis accepts a factor basis one of whose levels no row carries, so a basis swap inside a larger sampler can leave a level unobserved for a while, its column contributing nothing until a row returns to it; a numeric all-zero column stays refused, as at creation, where an empty column of either kind is refused. The alternatives were refusing the empty level on the swap as at creation, and accepting numeric zero columns too. Nothing breaks; the help for forest() and setForestBasis says which is accepted where. Not yet ruled on.
Record: commits 6b44f394 and f2142251. Marked: blank. [dec-C14]
